    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.399a26'>爾時，如來大悲愍故，遂去上衣現其身相。 告諸苾芻： 「汝等今者可觀佛身！ 汝等今者可觀佛身！ 何以故？ 如來、應、正等覺難可逢遇，如烏曇跋羅華。」 時，諸苾芻咸皆默然。</td>
    <td id='p369'>369. The Tathāgata then removed his upper garment and displayed his physical appearance out great sympathy. He told the monks, “Now all of you can look at the Buddha’s body! Now all of you can look at the Buddha’s body! And why? A <em>tathāgata</em>, <em>arhat</em>, and completely awakened one is as difficult to encounter as an <em>udumbara</em> flower.” The monks all remained silent.</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.399b1'>佛言： 「法皆如是，諸行無常。 是我最後之所教誨。」 作是語已，安心正念，入初靜慮。 從此起已，順次第入第二靜慮⋯乃至⋯ 非想非非想處及滅受想定，寂然宴默。</td>
    <td id='p370'>370. The Buddha said, “Such is the Dharma that actions are impermanent. That is my final instruction.” After saying this, he calmed his mind with proper mindfulness and entered the first meditation. From there, he emerged and continued next to the second meditation … <em>up to</em> … the abode that’s neither conception nor not conception and then the <em>samādhi</em> of the cessation of feeling and conception, where he peacefully remained in silence.</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.399b4'>時阿難陀問尊者阿尼盧陀曰： 「今我大師為入涅槃為未入耶？」</td>
    <td id='p371'>371. Ānanda then asked Venerable Aniruddha, “Has the great teacher entered <em>nirvāṇa</em>, or has he not done so yet?”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.399b6'>答曰： 「佛未涅槃。 但住滅受想定。」</td>
    <td id='p372'>372. He replied, “The Buddha has yet to <em>nirvāṇa</em>. He is just abiding in the <em>samādhi</em> of the cessation of feeling and conception.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.399b7'>阿難陀言： 「我曾從佛親聞此語： 『若佛世尊入邊際定，寂然不動，從此無間世間眼閉必入涅槃。』」</td>
    <td id='p373'>373. Ānanda said, “I once heard the Buddha say this: ‘If the Buddha, the Bhagavān, enters the endpoint <em>samādhi</em>, and he is peaceful and unmoving, he will surely enter <em>nirvāṇa</em> from there, and the world’s eye will be immediately shut.’”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.399b9'>爾時，世尊從滅受想定出，逆次第入非想非非想處。 從非想非非想出，入無所有處。 次入識無邊處，次入空無邊處，次入第四靜慮，入第三、入第二、入初靜慮。 從初禪出，還入第二、第三、第四靜慮，寂然不動。 便入無餘妙涅槃界。</td>
    <td id='p374'>374. The Bhagavān then emerged from the <em>samādhi</em> of the cessation of feeling and conception and reversed course to enter the abode that’s neither conception nor not conception. He emerged from neither conception nor not conception and entered the abode of nothingness. He next entered the measureless abode of awareness, next the measureless abode of emptiness, next the fourth meditation, the third meditation, the second meditation, and the first meditation. He emerged from the first meditation and entered the second, third, and fourth meditation, where he was peaceful and unmoving. He then entered the realm of <em>nirvāṇa</em> without remainder.</td>
  </tr>
<tr>
  <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.399b15'></td>
  <td class='subheading'>40. King Ajātaśatru Learns the Buddha Has Entered Nirvāṇa</td>
</tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.399b15'>爾時，世尊纔涅槃後，大地震動，流星晝現，諸方熾然，於虛空中諸天擊鼓。 時具壽大迦攝波，在王舍城羯蘭鐸迦池竹林園中。 見大地動，即便斂念觀察：「何事？」 便見如來入大圓寂，自念： 「我今既無大師。 唯依法住。 諸行法爾知更云何？」 復作是念： 「此未生怨王勝身之子信根初發。 彼若聞佛入涅槃者，必嘔熱血而死！ 我今宜可預設方便。」</td>
    <td id='p375'>375. Right after the Bhagavān’s <em>nirvāṇa</em>, there was an earthquake and stars fell that made it look like daytime, their fires burning is all directions, and the gods beat drums in the sky. At the time, the elder Mahākāśyapa was at Kalandaka Pond in the bamboo park of Rājagṛha. He witnessed the earthquake and instantly looked up, thinking, “What has happened?” Then he saw the Tathāgata enter the great and perfect peace and thought to himself, “Now I will not have the great teacher. I will have to rely on what Dharma remains. How will we know the law of actions is so?” He also thought, “This superior son Ajātaśatru has just initiated his faculty of faith. If he hears that the Buddha has entered <em>nirvāṇa</em>, he will surely vomit hot blood and die! Now I ought to make a plan for this in advance.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.399b22'>作是念，已即命城中行雨大臣： 「仁今知不？ 佛已涅槃。 未生怨王信根初發，彼若聞佛入涅槃者，必嘔熱血而死！ 我今宜可預設方便。」</td>
    <td id='p376'>376. Having had that thought, he then ordered the prime minister Varṣākāra, “Did you know, sir? The Buddha has <em>nirvāṇa</em>-ed. King Ajātaśatru just initiated his faculty of faith. When he hears that the Buddha entered <em>nirvāṇa</em>, he will surely vomit hot blood and die! Now we ought to make a plan for this in advance.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.399b25'>即依次第而為陳說： 「仁今疾可詣一園中，於妙堂殿如法圖畫佛本因緣。 菩薩昔在覩史天宮， 將欲下生觀其五事。 欲界天子三淨母身。 作象子形託生母腹。 既誕之後，踰城出家。 苦行六年，坐金剛座，菩提樹下成等正覺。</td>
    <td id='p377'>377. He then narrated a sequence of events: “Now, sir, we can make haste to a park and create murals of the Buddha’s life in a marvelous palace hall. The <em>bodhisattva</em> was once in a Tuṣita Heaven palace. When he was about to descend to be born [on Earth], he looked for five criteria. The desire realm gods have mothers who possess three physical purities. He took the form of an elephant and was born from his mother’s side. Later, after he grew up, he escaped the city and left home. He practiced asceticism for six years, sat on the diamond seat, and achieved complete awakening under the <em>bodhi</em> tree.</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.399c1'>「次至婆羅痆斯國，為五苾芻，三轉、十二行四諦法輪。 次於室羅伐城，為人天眾，現大神通。 次往三十三天為母摩耶廣宣法要，寶階三道下贍部洲，於僧羯奢城人天渴仰。 於諸方國在處化生，利益既周將趣圓寂。 遂至拘尸那城娑羅雙樹，北首而臥入大涅槃。</td>
    <td id='p378'>378. “Next, he went to Bārāṇasī and turned the Dharma wheel of the four noble truths three times in twelve steps for the five monks. Next, he went to Rājagṛha to display great miracles for assemblies of humans and gods. Next, he went to the Trāyastriṃśa Heaven to preach the essentials of the Dharma for his mother Māyā, took a treasure staircase down to Jambudvīpa, and humans and gods looked longingly at him at the city of Kāśi. He magically appeared in different regions and countries, benefiting others by leading them to complete peace. On his way to Kuśinagarī, he laid down between a pair of sal trees with his head to the north and entered the great <em>nirvāṇa</em>.</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.399c7'>「如來一代所有化迹既圖畫已，次作八函與人量等，置於堂側。 前七函內滿置生酥，第八函中安牛頭栴檀香水。 若因駕出，可白王言： 『暫迂神駕躬，詣芳園所，觀其圖畫。』</td>
    <td id='p379'>379. “Once the murals of these events from one life of the Tathāgata are painted, the next step is to make eight boxes the size of a man and place them along the side of the hall. The first seven boxes are to be filled with fresh curd, and the eighth is to be filled up with water scented with oxhead sandalwood. If he has reason to go out in his chariot, you can tell the king, ‘Take a moment to go around in your miraculous chariot and head over to the sweet-smelling park and look at the murals there!’</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.399c11'>「時王見已，問行雨言： 『此述何事？』 彼即次第為王，陳說一如圖畫。 始從覩史降身母胎，終至雙林北首而臥。 王聞是語，即便悶絕，宛轉于地。 可速移入第一函中。 如是一、二、三、四⋯乃至⋯第七。 後置香水，王便穌息。」</td>
    <td id='p380'>380. “After he sees them, the king will ask Varṣākāra, ‘What story does this tell?’ Then you will explain each mural for the king in order. Begin with his descent from Tuṣita into his mother’s womb, and end with him lying between a pair of sal trees with his head to the north. Hearing this, the king will collapse and twist and turn on the ground. You can quickly move him into the first box. Like the first, put him in the second, third, fourth … <em>up to</em> … and the seventh box. Finally, put the king in the fragrant water, and then he will calm down.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.399c15'>是時尊者次第教已往拘尸那城。 行雨大臣一如尊者所教之事次第作已，時王因出大臣白言： 「願王暫迂神駕遊觀園中。」</td>
    <td id='p381'>381. After giving these instructions, the venerable then went to Kuśinagarī. Once Varṣākāra had done what the venerable had instructed in that order, the king had cause to go out, and the prime minister said, “May it please the king to take a moment and tour the park with his miraculous chariot!”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.399c18'>王至園所，見彼堂中圖畫新異。 始從初誕⋯乃至⋯倚臥雙林。 王問臣曰： 「豈可世尊入涅槃耶？」 是時行雨默然無對。 王見是已，知佛涅槃，即便號咷悶絕，宛轉于地。 臣即移舉置酥函中，如是至七方投香水。 從此已後，王漸穌息。</td>
    <td id='p382'>382. The king went to the park and saw the murals in the hall fresh and marvelous. Beginning with the Buddha maturing, he reached the Buddha lying between a pair of trees. The king asked his minister, “Could it be that the Bhagavān has entered <em>nirvāṇa</em>?” Varṣākāra remained silent and didn’t answer him. Seeing this, the king knew the Buddha had <em>nirvāṇa</em>-ed, and then he cried out and fell down, twisting and turning on the ground. His minister then moved him to the box with cream until the seventh box, when he was tossed into the fragrant water. After this was done, the king gradually calmed down.</td>
  </tr>
<tr>
  <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.399c24'></td>
  <td class='subheading'>41. Eulogies for the Buddha</td>
</tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.399c24'>爾時如來入涅槃時，娑羅雙樹名華下散彌覆金軀。 時有苾芻見斯事已，而說頌曰：</td>
    <td id='p383'>383. When the Tathāgata entered <em>nirvāṇa</em>, named flowers fell down onto his golden body between that pair of sal trees. When a monk saw this happen, he spoke in verse:</td>
  </tr>
<tr>
  <td title='t1451.24.399c26'><ul class='verse'>
    <li class='ch'>「世尊涅槃時，<br/>
    最勝娑羅樹，<br/>
    低枝下垂蔭，<br/>
    復散以名華。」</li>
  </ul></td>
  <td><ul class='verse'>
    <li>“When the Bhagavān <em>nirvāṇa</em>-ed,<br/>
    The most excellent of sal trees,<br/>
    Lowered their branches to shade him,<br/>
    And flowers were strewn over him.”</li>
  </ul></td>
</tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.399c28'>時天帝釋亦說頌曰：</td>
    <td id='p384'>384. Śakra the Lord of Gods also spoke in verse:</td>
  </tr>
<tr>
  <td title='t1451.24.399c29'><ul class='verse'>
    <li class='ch'>「『諸行無常：』<br/>
    是生滅法；<br/>
    生滅滅已，<br/>
    寂滅為樂。」</li>
  </ul></td>
  <td><ul class='verse'>
    <li>“‘Actions are impermanent:’<br/>
    This is the principle of arising and ceasing.<br/>
    Once arising and ceasing has ceased,<br/>
    That’s the happiness of peaceful cessation.”</li>
  </ul></td>
</tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400a2'>時梵天王亦說頌曰：</td>
    <td id='p385'>385. Then the king of the Brahma Heaven also spoke in verse:</td>
  </tr>
<tr>
  <td title='t1451.24.400a3'><ul class='verse'>
    <li class='ch'>「於一切世間<br/>
    生者皆歸死。<br/>
    無常力最大，<br/>
    諸行盡淪亡。</li>
  </ul></td>
  <td><ul class='verse'>
    <li>“Everyone in the world<br/>
    Who is born returns to death.<br/>
    Greatest is the power of impermanence,<br/>
    And one’s actions are entirely lost.</li>
  </ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
  <td title='t1451.24.400a5'><ul class='verse'>
    <li class='ch'>大師世間眼；<br/>
    十力無與等。<br/>
    化緣既周遍，<br/>
    寂滅在雙林。」</li>
  </ul></td>
  <td><ul class='verse'>
    <li>The great teacher was the world’s eye;<br/>
    His ten powers were unequaled.<br/>
    He was an agent of change everywhere,<br/>
    And then he peacefully ceased between two trees.”</li>
  </ul></td>
</tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400a7'>爾時尊者阿尼盧陀亦說頌曰：</td>
    <td id='p387'>387. Then, Venerable Aniruddha also spoke in verse:</td>
  </tr>
<tr>
  <td title='t1451.24.400a8'><ul class='verse'>
    <li class='ch'>「佛無出入息，<br/>
    其心亦湛然。<br/>
    世眼今已閉，<br/>
    寂然安不動。</li>
  </ul></td>
  <td><ul class='verse'>
    <li>“The Buddha is not inhaling or exhaling;<br/>
    His mind is also placid.<br/>
    The world’s eye has now been shut.<br/>
    He is tranquil, calm and unmoving.</li>
  </ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
  <td title='t1451.24.400a10'><ul class='verse'>
    <li class='ch'>世尊十力具<br/>
    化盡入無餘。<br/>
    見聞諸有情，<br/>
    毛竪心驚怖。</li>
  </ul></td>
  <td><ul class='verse'>
    <li>The Bhagavān who possessed ten powers<br/>
    Has converted everyone and entered without remainder.<br/>
    Sentient beings who see and hear of it<br/>
    Get goosebumps and are shocked.</li>
  </ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
  <td title='t1451.24.400a12'><ul class='verse'>
    <li class='ch'>汝心莫沈沒，<br/>
    亦勿懷憂惱。<br/>
    佛證真木叉，<br/>
    譬如燈焰滅。」</li>
  </ul></td>
  <td><ul class='verse'>
    <li>Don’t let your minds be drowned,<br/>
    nor harbor any grief.<br/>
    The Buddha realized the true release,<br/>
    Like a burning lamp that was doused.”</li>
  </ul></td>
</tr>
<tr>
  <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400a14'></td>
  <td class='subheading'>42. Aniruddha Counsels the Monks</td>
</tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400a14'>時諸苾芻見佛世尊般涅槃已，各懷悲感。 或有迷悶宛轉于地。 椎胸大喚心生憂慘。 或有尋思法理，作如是說： 「我等今時宜自裁忍。 世尊常說： 『一切光華可愛樂事，雖是尊重，終歸無常，悉皆離別。』」</td>
    <td id='p388'>388. Then, having witnessed the Buddha, the Bhagavān, <em>parinirvāṇa</em>, the monks each felt quite sad. Some of them were distraught and fell down, twisting and turning on the ground. Some beat their chest and yelled loudly, feeling aggrieved. Some searched for the Dharma’s principle and said this: “Now, it would be better that we endure this ourselves. The Bhagavān always said, ‘Although honored, all bright flowers that are lovely and pleasant things return in the end to impermanence, which separates us from them.’”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400a19'>時阿尼盧陀告阿難陀曰： 「具壽，宜應勸誘大眾，且各裁抑，勿乖儀式，莫大悲號。 所以者何？ 於此現有住百千劫長壽諸天，皆生嫌恥。 作如是語： 『云何苾芻於佛世尊善說法律而為出家，不能善觀諸無常事，乃生憂苦？』」</td>
    <td id='p389'>389. Aniruddha then told Ānanda, “Elder, we should encourage the great assembly so they each are restrained, not impolite, and not crying loudly. Why is that? Gods who have lived for a hundred thousand eons have appeared here, and they are disgusted and embarrassed. They are saying, ‘How is it that these monks who left home to be skillfully taught the Dharma and Vinaya by the Buddha, the Bhagavān, cannot skillfully contemplate the impermanence of things and become so anguished?’”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400a23'>阿難陀白言： 「此諸天眾其數幾何？」</td>
    <td id='p390'>390. Ānanda asked, “How many gods are gathered here?”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400a24'>答曰：「從此拘尸那城，乃至金河及娑羅雙樹，至壯士繫冠制底，於此四邊周十二踰繕那，大威德天悉皆充滿。 無有空隙，可容立杖。 而此諸天見佛涅槃。 各懷悲感，椎胸懊惱，悶絕于地。 亦有如前，共相開解，且各裁止⋯乃至⋯『終歸無常，悉皆離別。』」</td>
    <td id='p391'>391. He answered, “From Kuśinagarī to the Golden River and this pair of sal trees to the Malla’s Makuṭabandhana Shrine, there are great and dignified gods filling an area of twelve <em>yojana</em>s. There’s no empty space between them, not even enough for a staff. These gods have seen the Buddha <em>nirvāṇa</em>. Each of them were aggrieved, beat their chests, became distraught, and fell to the ground. As before, they each understood and restrained themselves … <em>up to</em> … ‘in the end return to impermanence, which separates us from them.’”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400b1'>于時尊者阿尼盧陀為阿難陀及諸大眾廣說法要乃至天明，時苾芻等默然聽受。</td>
    <td id='p392'>392. Venerable Aniruddha then explained the essentials of the Dharma in detail for Ānanda and the great congregation until dawn, and then the monks were quiet and listened to him.</td>
  </tr>
<tr>
  <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400b2'></td>
  <td class='subheading'>43. The Mallas Move the Buddha’s Body</td>
</tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400b2'>阿尼盧陀復告阿難陀曰： 「汝今宜往拘尸那城告諸壯士： 『昨於中夜如來大師已入無餘妙涅槃界。 仁等今時所應作者宜當速辦，勿為後悔。』 復重告曰： 『如來大師於汝城邑入般涅槃。 爾等云何不興供養報佛慈恩？』」</td>
    <td id='p393'>393. Aniruddha also told Ānanda, “Now, it would be fitting for you to go to Kuśinagarī and tell the Mallas, ‘Last night, the Tathāgata, the great teacher, entered into the wonderful realm of <em>nirvāṇa</em> without remainder. If you gentlemen will do what should be done right now, then it would be fitting for it to be carried it out quickly and not have regrets about it later.’ Say this to them as well: ‘The Tathāgata, the great teacher, entered <em>parinirvāṇa</em> near your city. That being so, how could you not bring offerings to repay the Buddha’s kindness and favor?’”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400b7'>時阿難陀聞是語已，即持天衣，將一苾芻以為侍者，往壯士集堂。 有五百人先在堂處。 尊者告曰：「仁等壯士及諸大眾：如來大師已於中夜入無餘依妙涅槃界。 仁等今時所應作者宜應速辦。 勿生後悔。」 又重告曰： 「如來大師於汝城邑入般涅槃。 汝等云何不興供養報佛慈恩？」</td>
    <td id='p394'>394. When he heard this said, Ānanda took his robe and brought a monk along as his assistant, and they went to a hall where Mallas had gathered. There were five hundred people in front of the hall. The venerable addressed them, “Gentlemen, Mallas and everyone assembled: The Tathāgata, the great teacher, has entered the wondrous realm of <em>nirvāṇa</em> without remainder in the middle of the night. Gentlemen, it would be fitting now to do what should be done and carry it out quickly. Don’t have regrets about it later.” He also told them, “The Tathāgata, the great teacher, entered <em>parinirvāṇa</em> near your city. How could you not bring offerings to repay the Buddha’s kindness and favor?”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400b14'>時諸壯士聞是告已，或有悶絕宛轉于地。 椎胸大喚，身體戰慄，不能自持。 或有高聲作如是語： 「我於佛所曾聞是說： 『世間無常，悉皆離別。』」 時諸壯士共相謂曰： 「宜各齎持種種華鬘、塗香、末香、燒香，及諸妙物音聲鼓樂。 速往雙林以申供養。」</td>
    <td id='p395'>395. After they were told this, some of the Mallas became distraught and fell down, twisting and turning on the ground. They beat their chests, yelled loudly, and their bodies trembled, being unable to control themselves. Some of them said this in a shrill voice, “The Buddha taught me what I’d never heard before, saying, ‘The world is impermanent, and everyone will be separated from each other.’” The Mallas then said to each other, “It would be fitting for each of us to stay chaste and bring a variety of flower garlands, rubbing incenses, powdered incenses, incenses to burn, and other fine things while singing and beating drums. We should go quickly to that pair of trees and give those offerings!”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400b19'>并大臣輔相各與眷屬男女大小親友知識，出拘尸城詣雙林所。 既至彼已，於佛臥處師子床前盡哀情已，各持所有上妙諸香名華，無數幢幡繒綵飲食奇珍，奏諸音樂廣供養已，白阿難陀曰： 「無上法王已歸圓寂。 不知今者葬禮如何？」</td>
    <td id='p396'>396. They went together with the prime minister and other ministers, who were each accompanied by a retinue of men and women, young and old, and their friends and associates as they left Kuśinagarī to visit that pair of trees. Once they had arrived, had felt pity for the Buddha lying there in front of them like a lion, and had each given all of their offerings of fine incenses, named flowers, countless banners, silks, meals, and precious gems while playing music, they then said to Ānanda, “The unsurpassed Dharma king has returned to the perfect peace. Do you know how he is to be interred and honored?”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400b24'>尊者告曰： 「然我先已奉佛教勅，所有葬法如轉輪王。」</td>
    <td id='p397'>397. The venerable told them, “Indeed, I received the Buddha’s instructions beforehand that the way he is to be interred is like a noble wheel-turning king.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400b25'>問曰：「其法如何？」</td>
    <td id='p398'>398. They asked, “What is the way to do that?”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400b26'>答曰： 「以白疊絮先用裹體，次以千張白疊周遍纏身。 置金棺中，盛滿香油，覆以金蓋。 積栴檀木及海岸諸香，以火焚燎。 後將牛乳澆火令滅，有餘舍利盛以金瓶。 於四衢大道建窣覩波。 周匝圍繞懸繒幡蓋，塗末燒香奏眾伎樂。 恭敬供養設大施會。 此是輪王焚葬之法。 如來大師倍勝於此。」</td>
    <td id='p399'>399. He answered, “First, his body is wrapped with white cotton cloth, and it’s wrapped all around with a thousand layers. His body is placed in a gold casket, which is filled with fragrant oil and covered with a gold lid. Sandalwood and coastal incenses are piled up and set ablaze to cremate him. Afterward, the fire is doused with cow’s milk, and a gold pot is fill with his physical remains.<sup id="ref59"><a href="#n59">59</a></sup> A <em>stūpa</em> is built at the intersection of a great road. It’s encircled and enclosed with hanging silk banners and canopies, rubbed, powdered, and burned incense, and musicians. People pay their respects, give offerings, and arrange great charitable events there. This is the way of interring a wheel-turning king. The interment of the Tathāgata, the great teacher, is double that.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400c4'>時諸壯士聞是語已，白尊者曰： 「我領其言，然非一、二、三日能辦此事。 若至七日住者，如前所為方可成就。」</td>
    <td id='p400'>400. After hearing this, the Mallas then said to the venerable, “We accept what you say, but these things cannot be arranged in one, two, or three days. If it can wait for seven days, then what you’ve said can be accomplished.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400c6'>答言： 「可爾。」</td>
    <td>He answered, “We can do that.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400c6'>是時諸人即便如前。 依輪王葬法一一備具無有闕少。 從拘尸那城周圍十二踰繕那，乃至繫冠制底，所有無量歸仰眾生咸來雲集。 各持香華種種伎樂供養之具。 壯士眷屬皆悉出城，詣雙樹間於師子床前，陳設所有盡心供養。</td>
    <td id='p401'>401. Those people then did as described. Based on the way to inter a wheel-turning king, they each provided what was required without omitting anything. In a twelve <em>yojana</em> radius around Kuśinagarī up to the Makuṭabandhana Shrine, there were measureless sentient beings gathered like clouds who had taken refuge. They each carried fragrant flowers and various musical instruments to give as offerings. The Mallas and their followers all left the city and went before the lion’s bed between a pair of trees where they arranged what they had, wholeheartedly giving their offerings.</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400c11'>時壯士中有一耆宿，告諸人曰： 「現在大眾，女持幢幡，男可擎輿。 我等齎持種種華綵、塗香、末香、燒香及諸音樂。 從拘尸那城西門而入，於東門出，度金沙河，至壯士繫冠制底。 勝處安置以火焚燒。」 是時諸人聞是語已，各各爭前欲舉金棺，雖共盡力竟不能動。</td>
    <td id='p402'>402. There was a senior among the Mallas who told the people, “Of the great assembly that’s present, the women can carry banners, and the men can lift the bier. We will bring a variety of flowers, silks, rubbed incense, powdered incense, and burned incense while singing. We will enter the west gate of Kuśinagarī, exit through the east gate, cross the Golden River, and go to the Makuṭabandhana Shrine of the Mallas. We will place his body at that excellent place and cremate him there.” Once they had heard this, each of those people attempted to lift his gold coffin, but they couldn’t budge it even when they used all their strength as a group.</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400c18'>爾時，具壽阿難陀白尊者阿尼盧陀曰： 「拘尸那城諸壯士等，雖竭筋力，竟不能動如來金棺。 我今不知有何所以？」</td>
    <td id='p403'>403. It was then that the elder Ānanda said to Venerable Aniruddha, “The Mallas of Kuśinagarī cannot budge the Tathāgata’s gold coffin even though they’ve exhausted their strength. I don’t know what reason there could be for that?”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400c20'>尊者告曰： 「此是諸天作如斯意： 欲令壯士及諸人民，女持幢幡、男捧尊輿，威儀整肅翊從如來。 我等諸天共持華、綵，燒眾妙香，奏天伎樂，廣陳供養。 於西門入，東門而出，度金沙河，至繫冠制底。 以是因緣威儀未備，不能移動。」</td>
    <td id='p404'>404. The venerable told him, “This is what the gods would like: They want to make the Malla women carry banners and the men to lift the honored bier with the dignity and seriousness of the Tathāgata. We and the gods will carry the flowers and silks, burn many sublime incenses, play heavenly musical instruments, and arrange broad offerings. We will enter through the west gate, exit through the east gate, cross the Golden River, and go to the Makuṭabandhana Shrine. They cannot budge him because the cause and condition of dignity has not been provided.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400c25'>是時具壽阿難陀報尊者曰： 「若如是者，可隨天意。」</td>
    <td>The elder Ānanda replied to the venerable, “If that’s so, we should do what the gods have in mind.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.400c26'>時諸壯士即隨天願。 備設如前方來持輿。 即便輕舉，捧戴而行。 于時，空中天雨嗢鉢羅華、拘物頭華、鉢頭摩華、分陀利華，沈水末香、栴檀末香、多揭羅多摩羅末香，及曼陀羅華等。 諸天伎樂百千萬種，於虛空中一時俱奏。 諸天華蓋其從如雲，并散天衣有盈億數。</td>
    <td id='p405'>405. The Mallas then followed the wishes of the gods. They made the arrangements as before and came to carry the bier. They lifted it gently, and carried the bier as they walked. The gods rained blue lotuses, red lotuses, white water lilies, and white lotuses down from the sky as well as powdered agarwood incense, powdered sandalwood incense, powdered crape jasmine and cinnamon incense, coral tree flowers, and so forth. The gods played hundreds of thousands of tens of thousands of musical instruments at the same time in the sky. The heavenly flowers covered their followers like clouds, and they scattered heavenly cloth, which overflowed in the millions.</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401a3'>時拘尸那城諸壯士等各相謂曰： 「天供養已，我等應為。」 時諸壯士及餘一切貴賤男女，營辦香華威儀嚴肅，百千萬種不可勝紀。 恭敬供養隨從金棺城中，而過度金沙河至繫冠制底，所散之華積至于膝。</td>
    <td id='p406'>406. The Mallas of Kuśinagarī then said to each other, “The gods have given their offerings, so we should as well.” The Mallas and all the other men and women of upper and lower classes prepared incense and flowers that were dignified and solemn in the hundreds of thousands of tens of thousands of kinds too numerous to record. They paid their respects and gave offerings to the gold coffin in the city, then they crossed over the Golden Sand River and went to the Makuṭabandhana Shrine, where they scattered flowers until they piled up to their knees.</td>
  </tr>
<tr>
  <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401a8'></td>
  <td class='subheading'>44. Mahākāśyapa Encounters an Ascetic on the Road</td>
</tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401a8'>于時，有一外道梵志聞佛滅度，詣娑羅林。 持華數莖，還波波聚落。 於其中路，逢大迦攝波，與五百弟子威儀整肅。 將詣雙林禮大師足。 遇見外道問言： 「汝從何來？欲向何處？」</td>
    <td id='p407'>407. At that point, there was an ascetic from another religion who heard that the Buddha had been extinguished and went to the sal grove. He carried a many stemmed flower and was returning to the village of Pāpā. While he was on the road, he encountered Mahākāśyapa accompanied by five hundred disciples who were dignified and solemn. He was leading them to the pair of trees to bow at the great teacher’s feet. When they met that adherent of another religion, they asked him, “Where are you coming from, and where are you going?”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401a12'>外道答曰： 「我從拘尸那來，將詣波波聚落。」</td>
    <td>The adherent of another religion answered, “I’ve come from Kuśinagarī, and I am going to the village of Pāpā.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401a13'>迦攝波知而故問： 「汝從彼來，知我大師釋迦牟尼如來四大安不？」</td>
    <td id='p408'>408. Knowing the answer, Kāśyapa asked, “Do they know where you have come from that the four basic elements of my great teacher, the Tathāgata Śākyamuni, are at peace?”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401a14'>外道答言： 「我從彼來，親見大德喬答摩已入涅槃。 經今七日自滅度來。 所有人天皆以香華種種威儀，具申供養遺身舍利。 我從彼會得此華來。」</td>
    <td id='p409'>409. The adherent of another religion replied, “Where I’ve come from, I have personally seen that the Venerable Gautama has entered <em>nirvāṇa</em>. Seven days have passed now since his extinguishment happened. The people and gods who were there are all looking dignified and laden with offerings of incense and flowers of various kinds that they will deliver the remains of his body. I got this flower from that congregation.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401a17'>大迦攝波所將五百人中，有一莫訶羅苾芻。 稟性愚癡，不辯好惡。 聞外道語，遂出麁言： 「快哉！樂哉！ 我等從今免被拘制於諸戒律，云： 『此應作』、『此不應作。』 此事皆息。 自今已後，能持、不持。 皆由於我，可行者行，不須者棄。」</td>
    <td id='p410'>410. There was an elderly monk among the five hundred that Mahākāśyapa was leading. He was unintelligent, foolish, and couldn’t discern good from bad. When he heard this man of another religion say this, he made this crude comment, “What a delight! What joy! From now on, we will escape from being governed by the rules of discipline that say, ‘This should be done.’ ‘This should not be done.’ This duty has stopped. From this day forward, we can observe or not observe it. It will be up to us. Those who can practice it will practice it, and those who do not need it will discard it.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401a22'>時彼老叟出此語時，空中諸天聞其非法，即以神力，掩蔽聲響，不令人聞。 唯迦攝波領知斯語。</td>
    <td id='p411'>411. When that old man made this statement, the gods in the sky perceived its immorality and deadened the sound of his voice with their miraculous powers to prevent others from hearing it. Only Kāśyapa perceived his words.</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401a24'>是時尊者為教誨彼故，即於道傍暫時停歇，與眾俱坐。 告言： 「諸具壽，世間諸行皆悉無常，體不堅牢是難委信。 不得久存，並歸散滅。 宜起厭離，勿生愛著。 且止斯事。 我等速往，見佛全身，各並前進。」</td>
    <td id='p412'>412. The venerable then stopped on the side of the road and sat with the group in order to instruct them. He told them, “Elders, worldly actions are all impermanent, and the body’s infirmity is difficult to rely upon. It cannot last for long, for what comes together will be scattered again. One ought to become disenchanted with it and not become attached to it. Let’s put a stop to these things. We will go quickly to see the Buddha’s whole body, and each of us will go before it.”</td>
  </tr>
<tr>
  <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401a29'></td>
  <td class='subheading'>45. The Cremation of the Buddha’s Body</td>
</tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401a29'>時諸壯士并四眾等，先用疊絮裹如來體。 次以千張白疊周匝纏身。 置香油棺覆以金蓋。 各持香木如法焚燒，火不能著。</td>
    <td id='p413'>413. Then, the Mallas and the four assemblies first wrapped the Tathāgata’s body in cotton cloth. Next, they wrapped his body all around with a thousand layers of white cotton. They placed him in a coffin of fragrant oil and covered it with a gold lid. They each took fragrant wood and set it on fire according to the Dharma, but it wouldn’t catch fire.</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401b2'>時阿尼盧陀告阿難陀曰： 「雖欲然火終無著法。」</td>
    <td id='p414'>414. Aniruddha then told Ānanda, “Although they want to light the fire, it will never catch fire.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401b3'>問： 「其何故？」</td>
    <td>Ānanda asked, “Why is that?”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401b4'>答曰： 「斯為諸天不令火著。」</td>
    <td id='p415'>415. He replied, “The gods are preventing it from catching fire.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401b4'>復問： 「何緣？」</td>
    <td>Ānanda again asked, “For what reason?”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401b4'>答曰： 「為大迦攝波與五百徒眾隨路而來。 欲見世尊金色全身，親觀焚燎。 為待彼故天不令燒。」</td>
    <td id='p416'>416. He answered, “Mahākāśyapa and five hundred of his followers are on their way here. They want to see the Bhagavān’s golden body and to watch the cremation. The gods are preventing it from burning in order to help them.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401b7'>時阿難陀即以此事普告眾知，須臾尊者徒眾皆至。 拘尸那城諸人遙見尊者眾來。 各持香華種種音樂，詣尊者所頭面禮足。</td>
    <td id='p417'>417. Ānanda then announced this to the whole assembly, and it was at that moment that the venerable and his followers arrived. The people of Kuśinagarī saw the venerable and his assembly arriving from afar. Each of them carried fragrant flowers and various musical instruments as they went up to the venerable and bowed their heads at his feet.</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401b9'>時有無量百千大眾隨從尊者，詣世尊所。 除去香木啟大金棺，千疊及絮並開解已，瞻仰尊容，頭面禮足。</td>
    <td id='p418'>418. There was then a great assembly of measureless hundreds of thousands that had followed the venerable to the Bhagavān. Once they had removed the fragrant wood piled up on the great gold coffin and took off the thousand layers of cotton, they looked upon the sage’s countenance and bowed their heads at his feet.</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401b12'>於此時中，唯有四大耆宿聲聞，謂具壽阿若憍陳如、具壽難陀、具壽十力迦攝波、具壽摩訶迦攝波。 然摩訶迦攝波有大福德多獲利養，衣、鉢、藥直觸事有餘。 尊者作念： 「我今自辦供養世尊，」即辦白疊千張及白疊絮。 先以絮裹，後用疊纏。 置金棺中，傾油使滿，覆以金蓋。 積諸香木，退住一面。 由佛餘威及諸天力，所有香木自然火起。</td>
    <td id='p419'>419. During this time, there were only four great senior disciples: the elders Ājñātakauṇḍinya, Cunda, Daśabalakāśyapa, and Mahākāśyapa.<sup id="ref60"><a href="#n60">60</a></sup> Still, Mahākāśyapa had great fortunate virtue and had gotten many benefits, such as robes, bowl, and tangibles that could be traded for medicine remaining. The venerable thought, “Now, I will arrange offerings to the Bhagavān myself,” and then he arranged for a thousand layers of white cotton cloth and raw white cotton. He first covered the body in raw cotton, and then the body was wrapped in the cotton cloth afterward. It was then placed in a gold coffin, which was filled with oil and covered with a gold lid. They piled up fragrant wood on it and then withdrew to stand to one side. Because of the remaining dignity of the Buddha and the power of the gods, the fragrant wood spontaneously burst into flames.</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401b19'>時阿難陀右繞火積說伽他曰：</td>
    <td id='p420'>420. Ānanda then circled the fire clockwise and spoke in verse:</td>
  </tr>
<tr>
  <td title='t1451.24.401b21'><ul class='verse'>
    <li class='ch'>「如來妙體歸圓寂，<br/>
    自然火起燎餘身。<br/>
    唯留內外一雙全，<br/>
    所有千衣隨火化。」</li>
  </ul></td>
  <td><ul class='verse'>
    <li>“The Tathāgata’s marvelous body returns to perfect peace<br/>
    As the spontaneous fire burns the body that was leftover.<br/>
    Only the inner and outer layers remain intact,<br/>
    As the rest of the thousand layers of cloth become flames.”<sup id="ref61"><a href="#n61">61</a></sup></li>
  </ul></td>
</tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401b23'>時拘尸那城諸壯士等，欲以牛乳注火令滅，未瀉之頃其火積中忽生四樹： 一、金色乳樹，二、赤色乳樹，三、菩提樹，四、烏曇跋樹。 於此樹中，乳自流出，令火皆滅。</td>
    <td id='p421'>421. Then, the Mallas of Kuśinagarī wanted to douse the fire with cow’s milk, but suddenly four trees grew there before they could pour it onto the burning pyre. The first was a gold milk tree, the second was a red milk tree, the third was a <em>bodhi</em> tree, and the fourth was an <em>udumbara</em> tree. Milk flowed from these trees, which put the fire out.</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401b26'>是時，拘尸那城諸貴賤等共收舍利，盛金瓶中，置七寶輿上。 以種種香華栴檀沈水塗香、末香、燒香、繒蓋、幢幡、音聲、伎樂，廣陳供養。 舁入城中，安妙堂上。 復更如前，盛興供養。</td>
    <td id='p422'>422. The nobles and common people of Kuśinagarī then collected together his physical remains, filled a gold pot with them, and placed it on a bier made of the seven treasures. They arranged extensive offerings of fragrant flowers, sandalwood, agarwood, incense salves, powdered incense, burned incense, silk covers, flags and banners, music, and musical instruments. They carried the bier into the city and placed it on the marvelous hall. Once more, they filled the bier with offerings as before.</td>
  </tr>
<tr>
  <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401c1'></td>
  <td class='subheading'>46. The Standoff Over the Buddha’s Remains</td>
</tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401c1'>是時，波波聚落諸壯士等聞佛世尊於拘尸那城入般涅槃已經七日。 無量人天廣陳供養，於其聚落總集四兵，象、馬、車、步。 各自嚴辦種種器仗。 共詣拘尸那城，欲分舍利。 既至城已，報諸人曰： 「無上法王眾生慈父。 我等諸人比於長夜供養恭敬，親承訓導受持正法。 今既滅度，有餘舍利我等欲取。 將往波波聚落，建窣覩波安置供養。」</td>
    <td id='p423'>423. At that point, the Mallas of the village of Pāpā heard that seven days had passed since the Buddha, the Bhagavān, had entered <em>parinirvāṇa</em> near Kuśinagarī. Measureless people and gods arranged extensive offerings, and the fourfold army was assembled in that village, which are elephant, horse, chariot, and foot troops. Each of them was equipped with a variety of armor and weapons. They marched together to Kuśinagarī with the desire for a share of the Buddha’s remains. When they arrived at the city, they people there said, “The unsurpassed Dharma king was a kind father to sentient beings. We are the people who have given offerings and paid our respects during the long night, and we’ve personally received his guidance and maintained the proper Dharma. Now that he has been extinguished, we would like to take some of his remains. We will take them to the village of Pāpā and erect a <em>stūpa</em> where they’ll be placed and offerings made to them.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401c8'>城中諸人聞斯告已咸作是言： 「世尊導師是我慈父親承訓誘。 既於我界而般涅槃，全身舍利應留永劫於此供養。 終不分與外邑諸人。」</td>
    <td id='p424'>424. Once they had heard this, the people in the city all said, “The Bhagavān was a teacher who was our kind father who gave us personal guidance. Now that he has <em>parinirvāṇa</em>-d in our territory, the remains of his whole body will be kept here forever to be given offerings. They will never be divided and given to the people of foreign cities.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401c12'>時波波人遣使答曰： 「若分者善。 如不與者，我等當以強力奪取。」</td>
    <td id='p425'>425. The people from Pāpā then sent a messenger with this reply: “It would be good if they were divided. If they are not given to us, then we will take them by force!”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401c14'>城人聞已告彼眾曰： 「徒事鬪戰。 終不可得。」</td>
    <td id='p426'>426. Once they heard this, the people in the city told that group, “Going to war will be useless. That will never obtain them.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401c15'>爾時，遮洛迦邑、部魯迦邑、阿羅摩邑、吠率奴邑、劫比羅城諸釋迦子、薜舍離栗㚲毘子悉皆來集。 是時，摩伽陀國未生怨王既聞佛世尊於拘尸那城入般涅槃，一切人天廣設供養。 既聞是事生大憂苦，遂告行雨大臣曰： 「卿今知不？ 我聞世尊已入涅槃，在拘尸城大興供養。 為爭舍利諸處競來欲相侵奪。 我今亦往請取身骨。」</td>
    <td id='p427'>427. At that point, the Śākyans from the towns of Calaka, Bulaka, Ārāma, Viṣṇu, and the city Kapilavastu as well as the Licchavis of Vaiśālī all came and gathered at Kuśinagarī. Then, King Ajātaśatru of Magadha heard that the Bhagavān had entered <em>parinirvāṇa</em> near Kuśinagarī and that all the people and gods had arranged extensive offerings.<sup id="ref62"><a href="#n62">62</a></sup> Once he heard about these events, the king became distraught and told his prime minister Varṣākāra, “Did you know? I’ve heard that the Bhagavān entered <em>nirvāṇa</em>, and a great offering took place at Kuśinagarī. A dispute is happening between different localities who have come wanting to seize his remains. I will also go now and request his bones.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401c22'>臣曰： 「如是，應裝整兵，便往拘尸那城。」</td>
    <td id='p428'>428. His minister said, “Yes, an army should be organized and sent to Kuśinagarī.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401c23'>時未生怨王遂乘大象，欲往佛所。 纔昇象上，念佛恩深。 心便悶絕，從象墜墮，宛轉于地。 良久乃穌便乘馬去。 念佛恩故，不能抑止，還墮于地。</td>
    <td id='p429'>429. King Ajātaśatru then wanted to follow them on a great elephant to where the Buddha was. When he was hoisted up onto the elephant, he thought of the Buddha with deep affection. He felt so despondent that he fell off of the elephant, twisting and turning on the ground. After a while, he recovered enough to mount a horse and depart. Because of his affectionate thoughts of the Buddha, he wasn’t able to control himself and again fell to the ground.</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.401c26'>久穌息已，告行雨大臣曰： 「我今不能親往佛所。 卿等今者可領四兵，往拘尸那城，傳我言教。 問訊壯士： 『少病少惱、起居輕利、安樂行不？ 世尊在日接引我等，長夜慇懃是我大師。 今於仁等聚落入般涅槃，有遺舍利幸與一分，於王舍城作窣覩波。 冀申敬重，香、華、伎樂種種供養。』」</td>
    <td id='p430'>430. When he had recovered, he told his minister Varṣākāra, “I’m not able to go to the Buddha myself. The rest of you will need to lead the fourfold army to Kuśinagarī and relay my instructions to them. Ask how the Mallas are doing: ‘What are your minor ailments and troubles, are you getting up and around, are you living happily? The Bhagavān had spent the day guiding us, and for a long time we took care that he was our great teacher. Now that he has entered <em>parinirvāṇa</em> in your village, I have sent for a share of his remains to be given to us so that they can be brought to Rājagṛha and a <em>stūpa</em> erected there. Then we can pay our respects to it and give various offerings of incense, flowers, and music.’”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.402a3'>行雨白言： 「如王教勅。」</td>
    <td>Varṣākāra said, “As the king orders.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.402a4'>即嚴四兵詣拘尸那城，告諸壯士曰： 「仁等咸聽！ 摩伽陀國未生怨王問訊仁等⋯具說如前⋯ 世尊大師於我等輩，常為饒益令得安樂，可尊可敬。 今者於仁聚落入般涅槃，有遺舍利幸當與分，於王舍城建窣覩波，廣興供養。」</td>
    <td id='p431'>431. Then his equipped fourfold army went to Kuśinagarī, and they told the Mallas, “Gentlemen, listen! King Ajātaśatru of Magadha asks how you gentlemen are doing … <em>and so forth as above</em> … The Bhagavān was our great teacher whom we honored and respected because he always benefited and made us happy. Now, he has entered <em>parinirvāṇa</em> in your village, and I have sent for a share of his remains to be given to us so that they can be brought to Rājagṛha and a <em>stūpa</em> erected where extensive offerings will be made.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.402a9'>諸壯士曰： 「世尊誠是饒益安樂一切群生，可尊可敬。 然於今者在我聚落入般涅槃，有遺舍利王欲見分，此誠難得。」</td>
    <td id='p432'>432. The Mallas said, “The Bhagavān truly did bless all beings with happiness, which is honorable and respectable. However, now he has entered <em>parinirvāṇa</em> in our village, and this mission to obtain a share of his remains for the king will truly be hard to do.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.402a11'>時行雨臣告諸壯士曰： 「若其仁等能與者善。 如不見分我加兵力強奪將去。」</td>
    <td id='p433'>433. Then the minister Varṣākāra told the Mallas, “It would be good if you gentlemen could give them to us. If we do not see our share, then we will take it by force and go.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.402a13'>答言： 「任意。」</td>
    <td>They replied, “Do as you like.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.402a13'>時諸人眾悉皆大集闐噎城隅。 城中所有壯士男女並閑弓射，即便總出象馬車步。 嚴整四兵欲共七邑兵交合戰。</td>
    <td id='p434'>434. That the crowds of men all gathered together and made a rumbling noise outside the city. The Malla men and women in the city defended it by shooting arrows, and then their elephant, horse, chariot, and foot troops came out. The well-equipped fourfold armies of the seven towns joined the battle.</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.402a17'>根本說一切有部毘奈耶 雜事 卷第三十八</td>
    <td class="fasc_heading"></td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.402a20'>根本說一切有部毘奈耶 雜事 卷第三十九</td>
    <td class="fasc_heading"></td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.402a23'>三藏法師義淨奉　制譯</td>
    <td class="taisho_byline"></td>
  </tr>
<tr>
  <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.402a24'>第八門第十子攝頌 涅槃之餘 次明五百結集事</td>
  <td class='subheading'></td>
</tr>
<tr>
  <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.402a26'></td>
  <td class='subheading'>47. Equally Dividing the Buddha’s Remains</td>
</tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.402a26'>時有婆羅門名突路拏，在於眾內。 見此諸人欲爭舍利共相戰伐，恐有損傷違害佛教。 自執長幡以麾大眾。 告拘尸那諸壯士曰： 「仁等且止！ 今欲為君陳其損益。 我比曾聞此大沙門喬答摩氏，憐愍一切諸有情故，於無數劫熾然精勤，忍怨害事。 長時，苦己讚行忍辱，由是因緣成無上覺。 心行平等猶若虛空，於諸有情普皆濟度。 眾生福盡，捨棄涅槃。</td>
    <td id='p435'>435. There was then a brahmin named Droṇa<sup id="ref63"><a href="#n63">63</a></sup> was among the crowd. Seeing these people wanting to fight a battle with each other over these remains, he was afraid that harm would be done in contradiction to the Buddha’s teachings. He held up a long banner and waved it at the great crowd. Then, he told the Mallas of Kuśinagarī, “All of you gentlemen, stop! What we want now is for an official to weigh the gain or loss. I’ve already heard about this great ascetic of the Gautama clan who has suffered through countless eons of diligence and patience with enemies and injuries because of his sympathy for all sentient beings. For a long time, he took pains himself to praise the practice of tolerance, and it was because of such causes and conditions that he achieved the unsurpassed awakening. His mental actions were equitable like the sky, as he ferried over sentient beings everywhere. When the merits of those sentient beings are exhausted, they will abandon them for <em>nirvāṇa</em>.</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.402b4'>「息化以來纔經七日，即興兵戰誠是相違。 唯願諸人勿為鬪競！ 我為平分必令歡喜。 佛身舍利分為八分，各將供養饒益群生。 量舍利瓶願同惠我。 持還本國建窣覩波。」</td>
    <td id='p436'>436. “Now he has stopped converting people for just these past seven days, and armies are raised for war, which truly contradicts him. Please, let these peoples not do battle with each other! I will divide [the relics] equally, and then surely you will be delighted. The remains of the Buddha’s body will be divided into eight parts, and then each portion can be given offerings to benefit all beings. I will measure the remains with a pot with the aspiration for equality as my focus. Take them back to your home countries and erect <em>stūpa</em>s to them.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.402b8'>時拘尸那城壯士聞已，報言： 「可爾！ 然大師世尊長夜修忍，不為殺害⋯廣如前說⋯ 仁今順教為我平分。 斯為善事。」</td>
    <td id='p437'>437. Once they had heard this, the Mallas of Kuśinagarī replied, “You may do so! The great teacher, the Bhagavān, did indeed cultivate tolerance for a long night, and he did not kill or harm … <em>recited in detail as above</em> … Now, you gentleman, follow his teachings and divide his relics into equal portions for us. This is a good thing.”</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.402b11'>其婆羅門既蒙許可，即分舍利而為八分。 第一分與拘尸那城諸壯士等，廣興供養。 第二分與波波邑壯士。 第三分與遮羅博邑。 第四分與阿羅摩處。 第五分與吠率奴邑。 第六分與劫比羅城諸釋迦子。 第七分與吠舍離城栗㚲毘子。 第八分與摩伽陀國行雨大臣。 此等諸人既分得已，各還本處起窣覩波，恭敬尊重，伎樂、香、華盛興供養。</td>
    <td id='p438'>438. Once he had their agreement, that brahmin then divided the remains into eight portions. The first share was given to the Mallas of Kuśinagarī for giving extensive offerings. The second share was given to the Mallas of Pāpā. The third share was given to the town of Calaka. The fourth share was given to the region of Ārāma. The fifth share was given to town of Viṣṇu. The sixth share was given to the Śākyans of Kapilavastu. The seventh share was given to the Licchavis of Vaiśālī. The eighth share was given to Prime Minister Varṣākāra of Magadha. Once these peoples had received their shares, they each returned to their homelands to erect <em>stūpa</em>s, where they paid their respects, gave honors, and provided extensive offerings of music, incense, and flowers.</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.402b18'>時突路拏婆羅門將量舍利瓶於本聚落起塔供養。</td>
    <td id='p439'>439. The brahmin Droṇa’s hometown then erected a <em>stūpa</em> to the pot that measured the remains and gave offerings there.</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.402b20'>有摩納婆名畢鉢羅亦在眾中告諸人曰： 「釋迦如來恩無不普，於仁聚落而般涅槃。 世尊舍利非我有分，其餘炭燼幸願與我？ 於畢鉢羅處起塔供養。」</td>
    <td id='p440'>440. There was also a brahmin student in the assembly named Pippala who told the people, “The Śākyan Tathāgata’s favor had no limits, and it was near your village that he <em>parinirvāṇa</em>-d. I did not get a share of the Bhagavān’s remains, but could I please have the ashes from the cremation fire? A <em>stūpa</em> will be erected at Pippalavatī to them where offerings will be given.”<sup id="ref64"><a href="#n64">64</a></sup></td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.402b23'>時贍部洲世尊舍利乃有八塔、第九瓶塔、第十炭塔。 如來舍利總有一碩六斗分為八分。 七分在贍部洲。 其第四分阿羅摩處所得之者，在龍宮供養。 又佛有四牙舍利： 一在天帝釋處。 一在健陀羅國。 一在羯陵伽國。 一在阿羅摩邑海龍王宮。 各起塔供養。</td>
    <td id='p441'>441. At that point, the continent of Jambudvīpa had eight <em>stūpa</em>s dedicated to the Bhagavān’s physical remains, a <em>stūpa</em> to the measuring pot, and a tenth <em>stūpa</em> to his ashes. The Tathāgata’s remains were altogether 96 liters,<sup id="ref65"><a href="#n65">65</a></sup> which was divided into eight shares. Seven of those shares were in Jambudvīpa. The fourth share obtained by Ārāma was at a <em>nāga</em>’s palace for offerings. Also, there were four teeth in the Buddha’s remains. One is with Śakra the Lord of Gods. One is in Gandhāra. One is in Kaliṅga. And one was in the <em>nāga</em> king Sāgara’s palace of the town Ārāma. Each of them had a <em>stūpa</em> erected for offerings.</td>
  </tr>
  <tr>
    <td class='ch' title='t1451.24.402b29'>時波咤離邑無憂王便開七塔，取其舍利，於贍部洲廣興靈塔八萬四千周遍供養。 由塔威德、莊嚴、世間，天、龍、藥叉、諸人、神等，咸皆恭敬尊重供養。 能令正法光顯不滅。 有所願求，無不遂意。 (已下序王舍城五百結集事)</td>
    <td id='p442'>442. King Aśoka of the town of Pāṭali opened the seven <em>stūpa</em>s, took their remains, and distributed them to shrines in 84,000 locations throughout Jambudvīpa for giving offerings.<sup id="ref66"><a href="#n66">66</a></sup> Because the <em>stūpa</em>s are dignified, decorated, and worldly, gods, <em>nāga</em>s, <em>yakṣa</em>s, people, and spirits all pay their respects, honor, and give offerings to them. This enabled the proper Dharma to shine clearly and not disappear. There are none who do not think of it when they have aspirations to pursue. (<em>From here, the introduction to the council of five hundred in Rājagṛha begins.</em>)</td>
  </tr>
</table>

<hr/>

<h3 id="notes">Notes</h3>

<ol class="notes-list">
<li id="n1"><p>This is an extended excerpt from the <em>Kṣudrakavastu</em> of the <em>Mūlasarvāstivāda Vinaya</em> (<a href="https://cbetaonline.dila.edu.tw/zh/T24n1451_p0382b29" target="_blank">T1451.24.382b29-402c4</a>). As such, it serves as an eighth century witness to the Sarvāstivāda version of the <cite>Mahāparinirvāṇa Sūtra</cite>. Its direct parallels are <a href="https://suttacentral.net/dn16" target="_blank">DN 16</a>, <a href="../../../01_agama/dirgha/DA_2.html" target="_blank">DĀ 2</a>, <a href="https://cbetaonline.dila.edu.tw/zh/T0005_001" target="_blank">T5</a>, <a href="https://cbetaonline.dila.edu.tw/zh/T0006_001" target="_blank">T6</a>, <a href="https://cbetaonline.dila.edu.tw/zh/T0007_001" target="_blank">T7</a>, a <a href="https://suttacentral.net/sf245/san/waldschmidt" target="_blank">Sanskrit manuscript</a> edited by Waldschmidt, and another version found in the Tibetan translation of the <em>Mūlasarvāstivāda Vinaya</em>. There are numerous other partial parallels that appear elsewhere in various Buddhist sources. I will reference them in the notes below.</p>
<p>This excerpt is untitled in the source text. I have provided the traditional title “Great Parinirvāṇa Sūtra” (S. <em>mahaparinirvāṇa sūtra</em>). The objective basis of this is the title of the <em>sūtra</em> that was part of the Sanskrit <em>Dīrgha Āgama</em>, which was likely from the same tradition as this Vinaya text.</p>
<p>This introductory story about Ajātaśatru deciding to invade the tribal confederation of Vṛji is found in all the other versions of this <em>sūtra</em> except for T7, which begins with the events at Cāpāla Shrine.</p>
<p>This story about Ajātaśatru also occurs as independent <em>sūtra</em>s that include varying amounts of detail. <a href="https://suttacentral.net/an7.22" target="_blank">AN 7.22</a> and <a href="" target="_blank">T211 Ch. 36</a> include only the conversation between the Buddha and Varṣākāra. <a href="../../../01_agama/ekottarika/40/EA_40_02.html" target="_blank">EĀ 40.2</a> includes the Buddha’s adaptation of his advice to the Vṛji for the future Saṅgha to follow. Finally, <a href="../../../01_agama/madhyama/MA_142.html" target="_blank">MĀ 142</a> is a full parallel that includes the additional lists of practices that supplement the Buddha’s initial advice to the Saṅgha. [<a href="#ref1">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n2"><p>This version of the seven ways to not retreat most closely resembles the version found in Waldschmidt’s Sanskrit edition, which is likely from the same tradition. The lists in DN 16 and MĀ 142 are also very similar to this one. DN 16 appears to echo an early version of the Sarvāstivāda list, given how closely aligned it is with MĀ 142. Many of the items in those two versions match word-for-word, whereas this and the Sanskrit edition diverge from them at times. We can surmise that all four of these versions descend from a common source. The differences are usually added detail or tweaks in wording that make the practices of the Vṛji more applicable to the Saṅgha.</p>
<p>When we turn to the other six versions of this list that exist, they vary much more in both wording and meaning. In some cases, entirely different items are included. In other cases, a common theme is discernible, but it’s changed somewhat. A good example is the different versions of the item about relations between men and women. They range from prohibiting the violation or kidnapping of women to advice about maintaining polite relations between the two sexes. This shows that there were multiple versions of this story among the early reciters. [<a href="#ref2">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n3"><p>The Buddha mentions having taught this to the Vṛji in the past in DN 16, AN 7.22, the Sanskrit edition, and MĀ 142; i.e., in the Theravāda and Sarvāstivāda traditions. AN 7.21 depicts the Buddha teaching these practices to the Licchavis in Vaiśālī prior to the events in the <em>Parinirvāṇa Sūtra</em>, but it may be that this <em>sūtra</em> existed only in the Theravāda and Sarvāstivāda traditions. The Buddha does not mention it in any other version of the Parinirvāṇa story.</p>
<p>There was at least one other tradition that depicts the Licchavis receiving this teaching. In T7, the Buddha teaches these seven practices to the Licchavis instead of Varṣākāra. In that alternate version of events, he teaches them after the Buddha announced that he will be entering <em>parinirvāṇa</em>, and the Licchavis had come to pay their last respects when they heard about it. [<a href="#ref3">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n4"><p><em>women who are betrothed</em>. C. <span class="ch">授花許</span>, S. <em>mālāguṇaparikṣiptā</em>?. <span class="ch">授花許</span> literally means “given the flower of permission.” It seems to parallel the Sanskrit expression <em>mālāguṇaparikṣiptā</em>. It referred to a marriageable woman, which literally means “the garland string discarded.” My impression here is that it refers to women who are betrothed to be married but are not yet, given that the other examples are women who are married to another man. [<a href="#ref4">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n5"><p>This traditional way of ending a <em>sūtra</em> suggests that it was considered an independent story from what follows. This would agree with AN 7.22 and the story found in T211, which both end at the same point. [<a href="#ref5">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n6"><p>This section of the <cite>Parinirvāṇa Sūtra</cite> extends the original story of Varṣākāra’s visit to apply the Buddha’s advice to the Saṅgha. While it may have been natural for the Buddha to use the customs of his native country as advice for the future Saṅgha to follow, it seems that initially the teaching given to Varṣākāra was a separate discourse.</p>
<p>The Theravāda tradition preserves the clearest evidence of this. AN 7.21 is the original discourse given by the Buddha to the Vajjians. AN 7.22 is the story of Vassakāra’s audience with the Buddha, during which the Buddha recalled his advice to the Vajjians. AN 7.23 is the discourse to the monks that adapted the Buddha’s advice to the Vajjians for the Saṅgha. AN 7.24-27 present the additional four sets of seven practices that follow as separate discourses, and AN 6.11 presents the final set of six practices that concludes the section.</p>
<p>While AN 7.22-27 are arranged in the same order as they occur in DN 16, they may have originally circulated independently, for there are other lists with the same theme that are not included in DN 16, such as AN 6.22, 6.33, and 6.69. There may have been a pool of existing discourses on the subject from which the compilers of DN 16 drew for the material they included.</p>
<p>Outside of Theravāda sources, collaboration for this later addition of material is found in T211, which only parallels AN 7.22. EĀ 40.2 fuses content parallel to AN 7.22 and 7.23 together into a single <em>sūtra</em>.</p>
<p>MĀ 142 represents the full development of this section of the <cite>Parinirvāṇa Sūtra</cite>, for it combines the above two stories and a similar set of teachings as we find in AN 7.22-27 plus AN 6.11 into a single <em>sūtra</em>. Finally, when we compare the parallels in other versions of the <cite>Parinirvāṇa Sūtra</cite>, we find that they each have a different arrangement and number of lists added after the Buddha’s initial advice to the Saṅgha. All of this points to this material having been added in later eras by each tradition independent of one another. [<a href="#ref6">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n7"><p><em>good Dharma won’t diminish</em>. C. <span class="ch">善法無損</span>. It seems possible that <span class="ch">善法</span> was intended to mean the “good qualities” of the monks, which would make it a reiteration of their virtues growing (<span class="ch">福德增長</span>). But I’ve maintained the reading of the good teaching given the context of the advice to the Vṛji, where I think it means the good laws of society in general. The parallel for the Saṅgha would be the Buddha’s teachings and precepts. [<a href="#ref7">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n8"><p>Six more lists now follow that give the Saṅgha more ways it can ensure that its future will be prosperous. This creates a set of seven lists when we count the first list. The next five are lists of seven practices, and one more list of six practices concludes the section. This is one more list of seven practices than we find in DN 16.</p>
<p>Of the six additional lists that follow, the first and last have parallels in all the other versions. These two lists must have been included in a very old precursor. The third and sixth lists must also be quite old because each of them is missing in only one other parallel. The third list is missing from T6, and the sixth list is missing from T5.</p>
<p>The second, fourth, and fifth lists are the least certain because they lack parallels in several versions of this section. The second list has no parallel in DN 16, T7, or the Sanskrit edition. The fourth list is missing a parallel in T5, T6, MĀ 142, and the Sanskrit edition. And the fifth list lacks a parallel in DĀ 2, DN 16, T5, and MĀ 142. These three lists must have been added at a later time than the other four lists. [<a href="#ref8">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n9"><p>As mentioned above, this list of six practices is paralleled in some way in every extant version of this story. This particular version is the most verbose of the parallels, dedicating an entire paragraph to each item. Despite this expansion, this version does share significant phrases and keywords with DN 16 that are not found in other parallels. There is significant diversity found among the other parallels, too, suggesting that the original version of this list was very simple and brief. Each tradition has developed it with examples and practical context to varying degrees. [<a href="#ref9">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n10"><p><em>circular area</em>. C. <span class="ch">曼荼羅</span> (EMC muan-ḍă-la = S. <em>maṇḍala</em>), S. <em>maṇḍala</em>. I’ve translated the term here in its literal meaning, but it should understood that this was a sacred space, usually outdoors and fenced off for special use. [<a href="#ref10">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n11"><p><em>read and recite the </em>sūtra<em> canon</em>. C. <span class="ch">讀誦經典</span>. I’ve translated this as it would be typically understood in Chinese. Unfortunately, I don’t have an Indic parallel that would provide me with an idea of what <span class="ch">讀誦</span> translated. The Sanskrit edition abbreviates the section on kind speech. Generally speaking, <span class="ch">讀</span> meant to read aloud from a document, and <span class="ch">誦</span> meant to recite from memory. Assuming that this was a faithful translation of the Sanskrit, it would be evidence that this was a passage composed after writing was adopted by Buddhists in India. [<a href="#ref11">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n12"><p>This telling of the <em>sūtra</em> lacks any detail on the Buddha’s journey between Rājagṛha and Pāṭaligrāmaka. In DN 16, the Buddha gives several teachings between the end of his advice to the Saṅgha and his arrival at Pāṭaligāma. In DĀ 2, there is only a single brief teaching that occurs between these two points in the story. It seems plausible that the in between material may consist of later additions that served to fill out the Buddha’s final itinerary. [<a href="#ref12">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n13"><p><em>faults</em>. C. <span class="ch">過失</span>, P. <em>ādīnavā</em>, S. <em>ādīnavaḥ</em>. The S. reading of this word, according to Edgerton, is fairly strong: “misfortune, disaster, misery, evil.” The meaning of misfortune certainly fits what the Buddha describes in this list. Pali dictionaries have a weaker reading for <em>ādīnava</em>, where it can mean “danger, problem” or “disadvantage, drawback.” The C. translation here means “error, fault,” where <span class="ch">過</span> refers to mistakes of commission and <span class="ch">失</span> refers to mistakes of omission.</p>
<p>This list is essentially the same as the one in DN 16, being a little more verbose and having the second and third items in reversed order. [<a href="#ref13">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n14"><p><em>to fortify the town against an attack from north of the city</em>. C. <span class="ch">置城邑以自牢固將伐北城</span>, P. <em>pāṭaligāme nagaraṁ māpenti vajjīnaṁ paṭibāhāya</em>, S. <em>udyuktaḥ pāṭaligrāmakaṃ nagaraṃ māpayituṃ vṛjīnāṃ pratighātārtham</em>. Both the P. and S. readings make it clear that Varṣākāra was establishing defenses at Pāṭali to ward off attacks from the Vṛji. The C. is less clear because <span class="ch">將伐北城</span> could be read to mean “lead an attack on the north city” instead of “future attack from the north city.” The north city presumably would refer to Vaiśālī, which was to the north of Pāṭali. [<a href="#ref14">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n15"><p><em>may their names be praised as invocations</em>. C. <span class="ch">願稱彼名而為呪願</span>, P. <em>pāṭaligāme nagaraṁ māpenti vajjīnaṁ paṭibāhāya</em>, S. <em>teṣāñ ca nāmnā dakṣiṇām ādiśasva</em>. DN 16 does not depict Varṣākāra dedicating the merits of his gift to the local spirits, but the Buddha references this practice in the verses that he speaks after the meal. The same is the case with DĀ 2, except that there the Buddha describes this practice in prose.</p>
<p>It would seem that the text envisions the kind of invocations that fill the Vedas, which consist of priests inviting gods to their sacrifice by enticing them with poetic praise. Here, however, the offerings are given to the Saṅgha rather than to the gods directly, and the merits are transferred to the local gods and spirits. [<a href="#ref15">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n16"><p><em>the rosewood forest north of the village of Kuṭi</em>. C. <span class="ch">小舍村北升攝波林</span>, P. <em>koṭigāmo</em>, S. <em>kuṭigrāmake viharaty uttareṇa grāmasya śiṃśapāvane</em>.</p>
<p>DN 16 does not mention where the Buddha stayed at <em>koṭigāma</em>, but the Sanskrit edition agrees with Yijing’s text. It also explains Yijing’s translation of the village’s name as “small house” (<span class="ch">小舍</span>). <em>Kuṭi</em> literally means a hut or cabin in both S. and P., while the P. <em>koṭi</em> means “the end.” It seems that the P. name for this village preserves a Prakrit pronunciation change, which create a name with a new meaning. DĀ 2 preserves a further change from P. <em>koṭi</em> to <em>koli</em> or <em>koḷi</em> in its C. transliteration (<span class="ch">拘利</span>). It’s difficult to say which of these are the correct name, given that S. sources tend to be later translations of Prakrit texts.</p>
<p>It’s worth noting that while the Buddha teaches the four noble truths in Koṭigāma in DN 16, this does not happen here or in DĀ 2, suggesting that this is a later insertion. This is especially so because the Buddha in DN 16 gives a second teaching that aligns with the parallels. [<a href="#ref16">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n17"><p><em>this is moral conduct, this is </em>samādhi<em>, and this is wisdom</em>. C. <span class="ch">此是尸羅，此是三摩地，此是般若</span>, P. <em>iti sīlaṁ, iti samādhi, iti paññā</em>, S. <em>itīmāni bhikṣavaḥ śīlāny ayaṃ samādhir iyaṃ prajñā</em>.</p>
<p>Yijing chose to transliterate S. <em>sīla</em>, <em>samādhi</em>, and <em>prajña</em>, perhaps to emphasize these three keywords in their original language. After this line, he translates <em>sīla</em> as “observing (i.e., keeping) precepts” (<span class="ch">持戒</span>), <em>samādhi</em> as “settlement (or stability)” (<span class="ch">定</span>), and <em>prajña</em> as “wisdom” (<span class="ch">智慧</span>). These are all standard translations, but I currently transliterate <em>samādhi</em> because I have not settled (no pun intended) on an English translation. [<a href="#ref17">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n18"><p><em>in a grove outside of the town Nādikā</em>. C. <span class="ch">販葦聚落村外林中</span>, P. <em>nātikā </em> or <em>nādikā</em>, S. <em>nādikā</em>, T. <em>grong-khyer sgra-can gang-na-ba</em>.</p>
<p>There is apparently a tradition that this town’s proper name was S. <em>jñātṛka</em>, the P. cognate being <em>ñātika</em>. This is not supported by the parallels of this passage (including DN 16), which seem to agree on <em>nādikā</em>.</p>
<p>Yijing translates the town’s name as <span class="ch">販葦聚落</span>, meaning “the reed trader’s town.” He probably interpreted <em>nādikā</em> in a similar way as <em>tūlika</em>, which can mean “cotton trader” (<em>tūla</em> meaning “cotton”). In S., both <em>nada</em> and <em>nāḍa</em> can mean “reed” or “hollow stalk.” This seems the most likely explanation for his translation. The town’s name in his text likely read <em>nāḍikā</em> or <em>nādikā</em>.</p>
<p>The Tibetan parallel for this passage translates the town’s name differently: “the town” (<em>grong-khyer</em>) “that’s a noisy place” <em>sgra-can gang-na-ba</em>. The common meaning of S. <em>nāda</em> is “sound, roaring, crying, bellowing, etc.” So, again, we arrive at S. <em>nādikā</em> as the most likely reading.</p>
<p>Turning to DĀ 2, the name of this town is transliterated as <em>nada</em> (<span class="ch">那陀</span>).</p>
<p>Something to note, too, is that Prakrit pronunciations vacillated between <em>-t-</em> and <em>-d-</em>, and this is likely the reason we see the variant readings in Pali that use both consonants. The tradition that reads the town’s name as P. <em>ñātika</em> may have been a rationalization of the <em>nātika</em> reading. Overall, it’s unclear to me which of these readings was originally correct. [<a href="#ref18">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n19"><p>Yijing first transliterates S. <em>upāsaka</em> (<span class="ch">鄔波索迦</span>) and then translates the term as “close serving man” (<span class="ch">近事男</span>). The latter emphasizes the fact that the laity performed various services to the Saṅgha that the Vinaya prohibited monks and nuns to do for themselves. [<a href="#ref19">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n20"><p>These three groups of laymen occur in DN 16 in different numbers (fifty, ninety, and five hundred), and they are only mentioned by the Buddha at the end of his answer to Ānanda. Before that, he addresses the fates of twelve named people: One monk, one nun, one laywomen, and eight laymen.</p>
<p>DĀ 2 adds to the story by depicting Ānanda thinking about a group of twelve laymen who have passed away who are also named individually. In that version, the three groups of laymen who had perished were twelve, fifty, and five hundred in number. But Ānanda does not mention any monks, nuns, or laywomen.</p>
<p>The Sanskrit edition, which usually corresponds to Yijing’s translation fairly closely, is a cross between Yijing’s translation and DĀ 2. The monks who go to the town learn about thirteen laymen by name and additional groups of three hundred and five hundred laymen who have passed away. These three groups are non-returners, once-returners, and stream-enterers, respectively.</p>
<p>Clearly, this section of the story has been retold in varying ways, which must have occurred later in the various canonical traditions. Yijing’s edition is the simplest version, mainly in not listing any specific names of the deceased. On the other hand, the lists of names for the laymen in DN 16, DĀ 2, and Sanskrit edition have enough in common to assume it must be older material that has changed over time through phonetic corruption and the addition of more names in each tradition. [<a href="#ref20">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n21"><p>This succinct Dharma mirror teaching is actually more verbose than the version in the Sanskrit edition, which defines it as simply pure faith in the Buddha, et al. DĀ 2 and DN 16 both feature the assurance of a noble disciple that they will not be reborn in a bad destiny and describe their in the Buddha, Dharma, Saṅgha, and precepts. [<a href="#ref21">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n22"><p><em>Vaiśālī … mango forest</em>. C. <span class="ch">廣嚴城⋯菴沒羅林</span>, P. <em>vesālī … ambapālivana</em>, S. <em>vaiśālī … āmrapālivana</em>. Yijing translated the name of this city as the city of “broad adornments” (<span class="ch">廣嚴</span>).</p>
<p>He transliterates the name of the forest where the Buddha stays: <span class="ch">菴沒羅</span> => EMC. •əm-muət-la => BHS. <em>āmra</em> (“mango”) or perhaps <em>amalā</em> (“Indian gooseberry”).</p>
<p>This will also be the name of the woman who visits the Buddha and provides a meal for the Saṅgha. Yijing tells us in an interlineal note that she is not Āmrapāli (<span class="ch">奈女</span>), which suggests that his text had a variant name for her. The S. edition names both the grove and the woman Āmrapāli just as DN 16 does, so it is a bit mysterious. The story involving her doesn’t include the humorous between Āmrapāli and the laymen on the road found in DN 16 and DĀ 2, but otherwise it is not that different from the other parallels. [<a href="#ref22">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n23"><p>This interlude in which the Buddha warns the monks to be mindful because beautiful women were approaching is not found in every parallel of this story, but some variation on the theme is found in several. The Sanskrit edition is very similar to Yijing’s translation. In DN 16, the Buddha tells the monks to be mindful before the story of Āmbapāli begins, while the Buddha gives the same instruction when the Licchavi laymen approach in DĀ 2.</p>
<p>In T7, the Buddha tells the monks to contemplate the impurities of the body when Āmrapāli approaches, which was a traditional way of counteracting lust caused by physical beauty.</p>
<p>A curious detail about this version is that it says the assembly of monks numbered in the hundreds of thousands (<span class="ch">無量百千苾芻眾</span>). This certainly makes the whole episode look like an addition from a later era when assemblies were inflated to unbelievable sizes. This paired with the degree of variations between the parallels certainly argues for it to be a later addition. [<a href="#ref23">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n24"><p><em>Paiṅgika Māṇava</em>. C. <span class="ch">黃髮摩納婆</span>, S. <em>paiṅgika māṇava</em>.</p>
<p>Yijing’s translation of this brahmin’s name means “yellow hair.” The S. edition reads <em>paiṅgika māṇava</em>, but there it is not necessarily a name. The S. would more naturally describe the brahmin rather than name him: “a student of a follower of Paiṅgya.” Paiṅgya was the name of a Vedic philosophical tradition based on the teachings of a sage by the same name. S. <em>paiṅgika</em> was a name for a follower of this tradition among brahmins. Yijing may have interpreted the name literally as the tawny color of rats and mice, which was a possible reading of the word. A related word was S. <em>piṅga</em>, which could refer to the color of turmeric (deep yellow or orange).</p>
<p>It’s worth noting that this brahmin had a different name in DĀ 2 that was translated as “fully satisfied” (<span class="ch">并𩞚</span>). It is not impossible, then, that Yijing’s text read a little differently than the S. edition. I have adopted the S. reading for my translation, though, because it seems the most likely case to me. [<a href="#ref24">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n25"><p>This interlude of a brahmin praising the Buddha in Vedic fashion to the delight of the Licchavi men is not found at all in DN 16, but parallel verses are spoken by a brahmin with a different name in DĀ 2.</p>
<p>The Taisho 5 parallel features a brahmin with another name receiving a teaching from the Buddha before the Licchavi men invite the Buddha to a meal. In Taisho 6, a man with the same name as in DĀ 2 comes forward and speaks verses of praise.</p>
<p>That this element of the story doesn’t occur in DN 16 when the Theravāda tradition has frequent Vedic and Brahmanical references is also a reason to think this interlude is a later addition. [<a href="#ref25">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n26"><p>This teaching of five extraordinary things about the Buddha is missing from the S. edition, but this portion of the <em>sūtra</em> suffered damage, and it may be missing for that reason. In DĀ 2, the Buddha teaches the Licchavis a set of five rare treasures that are very similar to these five extraordinary things. The events there mirror those here, but they story is more succinct. [<a href="#ref26">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n27"><p><em>laid out seats and couches, put out a basin of clean water, chewing picks, [soap, cow dung, and fine earth]</em>. C. <span class="ch">敷設床席，置淨水盆、齒木、及屑</span>, S. <em>āsanakāni prajñapyodakamaṇiṃ pratiṣṭhāpya</em>, P. <em>khādanīyaṁ bhojanīyaṁ paṭiyādāpetvā</em>. The difficulty of this passage is the meaning of <span class="ch">屑</span>.</p>
<p>Other passages in T1451 indicate that it may have omitted a few terms. At T1451.24.219c10, we find this: <span class="ch">洗浴之時，須用齒木及澡豆、牛糞、土屑</span>. It presents a list of items used to clean up: chewing sticks, soap, cow dung, and fine earth. In our passage above, <span class="ch">澡豆、牛糞、土</span> is missing.</p>
<p>Another passage at T1451.24.265a23-27 explains how these things were used. It reads: “Since the great teacher had established that they were to always chew on tooth sticks, the monks would not eat while they were traveling if they found it difficult to find sticks. The Buddha said, ‘You should not stop eating. If there are no chewing sticks, you should use soap, fine earth, or dried cow dung. Thoroughly rinse with clean water three times, then eat as you like without worrying about it further.’” (<span class="ch">大師既制恒嚼齒木，苾芻道行卒求難得遂不敢食。佛言：「不應斷食。若無齒木，應用澡豆土屑及乾牛糞。以水三遍淨漱，隨意喰食勿復生疑。」</span>).</p>
<p>I have inserted the full list of cleaning items based on these passages. [<a href="#ref27">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n28"><p><em>Veṇuvana</em>. C. <span class="ch">竹林</span>, S. <em>veṇugrāmaka</em>, P. <em>veḷuvagāmaka</em>. Yijing’s translation of this place name was “bamboo forest,” and he indicates that it was in fact a forest by including the C. preposition <span class="ch">中</span> (“in, within, middle of”). The S. edition instead calls it <em>veṇugrāmaka</em> (“town of Veṇu”), where <em>veṇu</em> literally means “bamboo.” The P. reading in DN 16 might shed a little light on the situation: There, the town is called <em>veḷavant</em> or <em>beḷavant</em> (“full of bamboo trees” or “full of wood-apple trees”), which in compound form becomes <em>veḷavagāmaka</em> or <em>beḷavagāmaka</em>. It seems that in Yijing’s text, the name was read <em>veṇuvana</em>, and <em>grāmaka</em> was lost. The same is true in DĀ 2, which gives the place name as <span class="ch">竹林叢</span> (“copse of bamboo trees”), so this was not a spurious reading only in Yijing’s text. [<a href="#ref28">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n29"><p><em>peacefully extinguished</em>. C. <span class="ch">寂滅</span>. This expression might translate a verb form of <em>nirodha</em> or <em>nirvāṇa</em>. I lean toward <em>nirvāṇa</em>. [<a href="#ref29">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n30"><p>There is a Sanskrit edition of this story preserved in the <cite>Divyāvadāna</cite> (cf. <cite>Divine Stories</cite>, Vol. I, pp. 337-348), which was a collection of stories drawn from the <cite>Mūlasarvāstivāda Vinaya</cite>. It’s a larger version than this one or the Waldschmidt edition, which adds details not found in Yijing’s translation.</p>
<p>Overall, the parallel story in DN 16 is more verbose even than the <cite>Divyāvadāna</cite> version, while DĀ 2 presents a more succinct story. [<a href="#ref30">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n31"><p><em>second-floor hall</em>. C. <span class="ch">重閣堂</span>, P. <em>kūṭāgārasālā</em>, S. <em>kūṭāgāraśālā</em>. P. and S. sources call this hall <em>kūṭāgāra</em>, which means an apartment or room on the second floor of a building. The C. translation usually, as here, means “second- or multi-floor hall”. This might have been intended to describe a building that is raised up on stilts and required a climb up stairs or ladders to enter. [<a href="#ref31">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n32"><p><em>Cāpāla Shrine</em>. C. <span class="ch">取弓制底</span>. Yijing translated S. <em>cāpāla</em> as meaning “holding a bow” or perhaps “armed with a body” (<span class="ch">取弓</span>). While S. <em>cāpa</em> is a word for a bow, I’m not sure how he arrived at “holding” a bow. [<a href="#ref32">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n33"><p><em>the hall of regular alms</em>. C. <span class="ch">常食堂</span>, P. <em>upaṭṭhānasālā</em>, S. <em>upasthānaśālā</em>. The parallel passage in Waldschmidt reads the same as in DN 16, which is the term for a meeting hall. Yijing translates this term to mean a hall of “regular alms” (<span class="ch">常食</span>). He must have understood these meeting halls as places where monks would gather to receive their daily alms from donors. I’ve not found any evidence that his text read differently than DN 16 or the Waldschmidt addition, so this must have been an interpretative issue. [<a href="#ref33">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n34"><p><em>the village Kuṣṭha</em>. C. <span class="ch">重患村</span>, P. <em>bhaṇḍagāma</em>, S. <em>kuṣṭhagrāmaka</em>. There’s quite a bit of divergence between the various parallels on the name of the towns to which the Buddha after staying in Vaiśālī. The Sanskrit edition by Waldschmidt lists the town as <em>kuṣṭhagrāmaka</em>, which lit. means the “village of leprosy,” or perhaps <em>kuṣṭha</em> was intended to refer to a plant like snow lotus or variegated ginger. Yijing translates the town’s name as “serious trouble,” which was a synonym for “serious illness” (<span class="ch">重病</span>). More than likely his text read S. <em>kuṣṭha</em> like the Waldschmidt edition.</p>
<p>DN 16 and DĀ 2 both have different destinations for the Buddha as he departs from Vaiśālī for the last time (<em>bhaṇḍagāma</em> and “fragrance shrine” respectively). This suggests that his actual itinerary was not remembered and filled in by each tradition in its own way. [<a href="#ref34">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n35"><p><em>the city Bhoga</em>. C. <span class="ch">受用城</span>, P. <em>bhoganagara</em>, S. <em>bhoganagaraka</em>. Yijing again translates the name of this city as “what’s gotten and used.” This was a literal reading of S. <em>bhoga</em>, which could mean “enjoyment, use, possession, property” and many other related things.</p>
<p>DN 16 and DĀ 2 both agree with the name of this city, so it must have been a point in the Buddha’s journey that was well remembered. Both DN 16 and DĀ 2 say that the Buddha passed through three more villages before arriving there, while this version of the story expands it to ten more villages without naming them. The Waldschmidt edition lists the names of eight villages between Kuṣṭha and Bhoga. [<a href="#ref35">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n36"><p>The purpose of this second earthquake seems to be to signal to the audience that about three months has passed since the Buddha decided to enter <em>nirvāṇa</em>. It does not occur in DN 16 or DĀ 2, but this interlude is also found in the Waldschmidt edition. [<a href="#ref36">back</a>]</p></li>
<li id="n37"><p>This introduces a section that is parallel to the eight assemblies section that follows the eight causes of earthquakes in DN 16 and DĀ 2. It’s a little intriguing that this section is not, strictly speaking, a numerical teaching like it is in DN 16 and that it occurs after the Buddha explains the causes of a second earthquake.
